Um, yeah… I came in because I’ve been trying to kind of clean up my image and my routine, mostly for this arts board stuff I’m involved with, and I thought that would make me feel more together, but it’s sort of done the opposite.

I’m not totally sure. I wouldn’t say I’m physically exhausted all the time or anything like that. Sometimes I feel kind of drained when I’m staring at what I need to do, but I don’t know if that’s “low energy” exactly or just me getting in my head.

Like, I’ll sit there with my sketchbook open and then remember I still haven’t organized my supplies — which, honestly, I’ve been meaning to do for weeks because they’re all in this one bin from when I moved things around — and then I kind of lose momentum. But I haven’t really been tracking whether I’m more tired than usual.

It’s weird because I’m still doing the basic day-to-day things, but these little things I normally care about keep slipping.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, and would you say your mood has been consistently low during this time?\n\nPatient: Um… I think it’s been about two months? At first I thought it was just the usual stress of changing habits, but it’s sort of stayed around.\n\nAnd the mood… I don’t know if I’d say low every single minute, but it’s been there a lot of the time since then, like this background heaviness.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any trouble concentrating or making decisions lately, especially when working on your art or planning events?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, kind of. It’s just been more tense and heavy than I expected since I tried to clean up my image and routine.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling more tired or lacking energy more often than usual, even when you’re not physically exhausted?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I wouldn’t say I’m physically exhausted all the time or anything like that. Yeah… I guess not as much, but it’s weird because I still do care about it. Like, if I’m working on a design draft, part of me still likes the idea of it, but then I start thinking about how it’s going to look to the arts board, or whether I seem like I have everything together, and it kind of sucks the satisfaction out of it.

Like with the design drafts or the fair planning notes, I’ll sit down and I know what I’m supposed to do, but then I get stuck thinking about whether I’m seeming put-together enough for the arts board, and suddenly the actual task feels way bigger than it is.\n\nIt’s not like I can’t function at all — I’m still doing the basic stuff. But these smaller things I normally care about keep slipping, and then I feel guilty, and instead of catching up I’ll cancel plans or avoid dealing with it. It’s just been more tense and heavy than I expected since I tried to clean up my image and routine.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling more tired or lacking energy more often than usual, even when you’re not physically exhausted?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I wouldn’t say I’m physically exhausted all the time or anything like that. Sometimes I feel kind of drained when I’m staring at what I need to do, but I don’t know if that’s “low energy” exactly or just me getting in my head.\n\nLike, I’ll sit there with my sketchbook open and then remember I still haven’t organized my supplies — which, honestly, I’ve been meaning to do for weeks because they’re all in this one bin from when I moved things around — and then I kind of lose momentum. But I haven’t really been tracking whether I’m more tired than usual.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that you’re not really interested in your art or event planning anymore, even when you have the time or opportunity to do it?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s that I don’t care about it anymore, exactly. Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say I think I’m failing at everything, but with the arts board stuff, yeah, it kind of gets in my head.

Sometimes I feel kind of drained when I’m staring at what I need to do, but I don’t know if that’s “low energy” exactly or just me getting in my head.\n\nLike, I’ll sit there with my sketchbook open and then remember I still haven’t organized my supplies — which, honestly, I’ve been meaning to do for weeks because they’re all in this one bin from when I moved things around — and then I kind of lose momentum. Then I feel guilty about it, and sometimes I cancel plans instead of catching up. At first I thought it was just the usual stress of changing habits, but it’s sort of stayed around.\n\nAnd the mood… I don’t know if I’d say low every single minute, but it’s been there a lot of the time since then, like this background heaviness.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any trouble concentrating or making decisions lately, especially when working on your art or planning events?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, kind of. Like with the design drafts or the fair planning notes, I’ll sit down and I know what I’m supposed to do, but then I get stuck thinking about whether I’m seeming put-together enough for the arts board, and suddenly the actual task feels way bigger than it is.\n\nIt’s not like I can’t function at all — I’m still doing the basic stuff. But these smaller things I normally care about keep slipping, and then I feel guilty, and instead of catching up I’ll cancel plans or avoid dealing with it. It’s just been more tense and heavy than I expected since I tried to clean up my image and routine.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling more tired or lacking energy more often than usual, even when you’re not physically exhausted?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I wouldn’t say I’m physically exhausted all the time or anything like that. Sometimes I feel kind of drained when I’m staring at what I need to do, but I don’t know if that’s “low energy” exactly or just me getting in my head.\n\nLike, I’ll sit there with my sketchbook open and then remember I still haven’t organized my supplies — which, honestly, I’ve been meaning to do for weeks because they’re all in this one bin from when I moved things around — and then I kind of lose momentum. But I haven’t really been tracking whether I’m more tired than usual.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that you’re not really interested in your art or event planning anymore, even when you have the time or opportunity to do it?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s that I don’t care about it anymore, exactly. Like, I still care about the art and the fair stuff — maybe too much, honestly — but since I tried to clean up my image and routine for the arts board, I’ve just been more tense and kind of down than I expected.\n\nSo I’ll have time to work on design drafts or fair planning notes, and instead of doing them I’ll get stuck, or drop some little thing I normally would’ve handled. Then I feel guilty about it, and sometimes I cancel plans instead of catching up. It’s frustrating because it’s not like I’ve stopped wanting to do it, I’m still doing the basics, but it feels harder to stay on top of the parts I used to be more excited about.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’re not getting as much enjoyment or satisfaction from your art or event planning as you used to, even when you do manage to work on them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess not as much, but it’s weird because I still do care about it. Like, if I’m working on a design draft, part of me still likes the idea of it, but then I start thinking about how it’s going to look to the arts board, or whether I seem like I have everything together, and it kind of sucks the satisfaction out of it.\n\nAnd the fair planning notes — I’ll do some of them, but then I’ll notice I missed some small thing I normally would’ve handled, and I get guilty instead of feeling accomplished. Then I’ll end up canceling plans or putting off catching up, which makes it worse. So it’s not that I’ve stopped enjoying art completely, it’s more like since I tried to clean up my image and routine, everything around it feels tense and heavier than it used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’ve been moving more slowly than usual, or fidgety and restless lately, even when you’re not trying to be?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think I’ve really noticed that. Like, I’m not sure I would catch myself moving slower unless someone pointed it out.\n\nSometimes I’ll get up to do something and then end up sort of wandering around my room because I see, like, a pile of receipts or an art supply I forgot to put away, and then I’m doing three little things instead of the one thing I meant to do. But I don’t know if that’s fidgety, exactly, or just me being distracted.\n\nI haven’t had anyone tell me I seem slowed down or restless, though. At least not that I can think of.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling like you're not good enough or that you're failing at things, especially related to how you're presenting yourself to the arts board?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say I think I’m failing at everything, but with the arts board stuff, yeah, it kind of gets in my head.\n\nLike, I came in because once I started trying to clean up my image and my routine for that, I got way more tense and down than I expected. It’s… frustrating.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like nothing really interests you or gives you pleasure anymore, even things you usually like doing outside of the arts board stuff?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say nothing interests me anymore. That sounds… bigger than what I mean, I think.\n\nLike, I still like things outside of all that, or at least I think I do. Could you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Um, yeah… I came in because I’ve been trying to kind of clean up my image and my routine, mostly for this arts board stuff I’m involved with, and I thought that would make me feel more together, but it’s sort of done the opposite.\n\nI’m still doing the basics, like I’m not completely falling apart or anything, but I’ve been more tense and kind of down than I expected. And then I keep dropping these little things I normally care about—like fair planning notes, or design drafts I was supposed to work on—and then I feel guilty about it and end up canceling plans instead of just catching up. It’s frustrating because it’s not even huge things, but it keeps happening.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’re not enjoying things that you usually like to do, like working on your art or planning events?\n\nPatient: Yeah, kind of. I mean, I still care about the art stuff and the fair planning, it’s not like I suddenly hate it. But since I started trying to make myself look more put-together for the arts board, everything around it feels heavier.\n\nLike, I’ll have design drafts I meant to work on, or notes for the fair, and then I just… don’t do them, or I put them off. And then I feel guilty, so instead of catching up or going to meet people, I’ll cancel plans. Which is dumb, because those are usually the things I like, you know? It’s just been making me more tense and down than I expected.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how you feel when you're working on your art or planning events now compared to before you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Before, working on art or event stuff felt kind of energizing, like it gave me something to focus on. Now it still matters to me, but it feels heavier. Like I’ll sit down with design drafts or fair planning notes and suddenly I’m thinking about whether I seem organized enough for the arts board, or if I’m messing up some image I’m supposed to have.\n\nAnd then I get tense, and if I don’t get it done I feel guilty, and instead of just picking it back up later I’ll cancel plans or avoid people. It’s weird because I’m still doing the basic day-to-day things, but these little things I normally care about keep slipping.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, and would you say your mood has been consistently low during this time?\n\nPatient: Um… I think it’s been about two months? It kind of started when I decided I needed to overhaul my routine and how I present myself, like trying to seem more polished for the arts board. At first I thought it was just the usual stress of changing habits, but it’s sort of stayed around.\n\nAnd the mood… I don’t know if I’d say low every single minute, but it’s been there a lot of the time since then, like this background heaviness.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any trouble concentrating or making decisions lately, especially when working on your art or planning events?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, kind of. Like with the design drafts or the fair planning notes, I’ll sit down and I know what I’m supposed to do, but then I get stuck thinking about whether I’m seeming put-together enough for the arts board, and suddenly the actual task feels way bigger than it is.\n\nIt’s not like I can’t function at all — I’m still doing the basic stuff. But these smaller things I normally care about keep slipping, and then I feel guilty, and instead of catching up I’ll cancel plans or avoid dealing with it. It’s just been more tense and heavy than I expected since I tried to clean up my image and routine.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling more tired or lacking energy more often than usual, even when you’re not physically exhausted?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I wouldn’t say I’m physically exhausted all the time or anything like that. Sometimes I feel kind of drained when I’m staring at what I need to do, but I don’t know if that’s “low energy” exactly or just me getting in my head.\n\nLike, I’ll sit there with my sketchbook open and then remember I still haven’t organized my supplies — which, honestly, I’ve been meaning to do for weeks because they’re all in this one bin from when I moved things around — and then I kind of lose momentum. But I haven’t really been tracking whether I’m more tired than usual.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that you’re not really interested in your art or event planning anymore, even when you have the time or opportunity to do it?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s that I don’t care about it anymore, exactly. Like, I still care about the art and the fair stuff — maybe too much, honestly — but since I tried to clean up my image and routine for the arts board, I’ve just been more tense and kind of down than I expected.\n\nSo I’ll have time to work on design drafts or fair planning notes, and instead of doing them I’ll get stuck, or drop some little thing I normally would’ve handled. Then I feel guilty about it, and sometimes I cancel plans instead of catching up. It’s frustrating because it’s not like I’ve stopped wanting to do it, I’m still doing the basics, but it feels harder to stay on top of the parts I used to be more excited about.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’re not getting as much enjoyment or satisfaction from your art or event planning as you used to, even when you do manage to work on them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess not as much, but it’s weird because I still do care about it. Like, if I’m working on a design draft, part of me still likes the idea of it, but then I start thinking about how it’s going to look to the arts board, or whether I seem like I have everything together, and it kind of sucks the satisfaction out of it.\n\nAnd the fair planning notes — I’ll do some of them, but then I’ll notice I missed some small thing I normally would’ve handled, and I get guilty instead of feeling accomplished. Then I’ll end up canceling plans or putting off catching up, which makes it worse. So it’s not that I’ve stopped enjoying art completely, it’s more like since I tried to clean up my image and routine, everything around it feels tense and heavier than it used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’ve been moving more slowly than usual, or fidgety and restless lately, even when you’re not trying to be?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think I’ve really noticed that. Like, I’m not sure I would catch myself moving slower unless someone pointed it out.\n\nSometimes I’ll get up to do something and then end up sort of wandering around my room because I see, like, a pile of receipts or an art supply I forgot to put away, and then I’m doing three little things instead of the one thing I meant to do. But I don’t know if that’s fidgety, exactly, or just me being distracted.\n\nI haven’t had anyone tell me I seem slowed down or restless, though. At least not that I can think of.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling like you're not good enough or that you're failing at things, especially related to how you're presenting yourself to the arts board?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say I think I’m failing at everything, but with the arts board stuff, yeah, it kind of gets in my head.\n\nLike, I came in because once I started trying to clean up my image and my routine for that, I got way more tense and down than I expected. I’m still doing the basic things, it’s not like I’ve totally fallen apart, but I keep dropping these little things I normally care about — fair planning notes, design draft details, stuff like that — and then I feel really guilty.\n\nAnd then instead of just catching up, I’ll cancel plans or avoid dealing with it for a bit, which makes me feel even more like I’m not presenting myself the way I should. It’s… frustrating.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like nothing really interests you or gives you pleasure anymore, even things you usually like doing outside of the arts board stuff?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say nothing interests me anymore. That sounds… bigger than what I mean, I think.\n\nLike, I still like things outside of all that, or at least I think I do. Sometimes I’ll start doing something random, like looking at little apartment decor ideas or watching someone paint online, and then somehow I’m thinking about whether my own workspace looks messy, which is not really the point. Sorry, that’s kind of a side thing.\n\nBut no, I haven’t really noticed that absolutely nothing gives me pleasure. It’s more hard for me to separate what I actually enjoy from all the pressure around looking like I’m doing okay.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself losing interest in almost all activities, even the ones you usually enjoy outside of your art and the fair planning?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure it’s almost all activities, no. I mean… I still get pulled into things, like if I see a video about someone making tiny ceramics or reorganizing a shelf in a really satisfying way, I’ll watch it longer than I meant to.\n\nBut then I start thinking, “Oh, my shelves look kind of chaotic,” and then it turns into this whole thing in my head instead of just enjoying it. So I guess I’m not sure if that counts as losing interest, or just not being able to stay with the enjoyment without it turning into… I don’t know, another comparison thing.\n\nI haven’t noticed that everything feels flat, though.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like you don’t really care about doing most of the things you used to enjoy, even outside of your art and the fair planning?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s most things, exactly. Or… I’m not sure. I still care about stuff in flashes, like if a friend sends me a weird thrifted lamp or some cute room setup, I’ll have opinions immediately.\n\nBut then I start drifting into, like, “Should I be making my place look more pulled together?” And that’s not really the same as not caring, I guess. It just gets tangled up. So I wouldn’t say I don’t care about things I used to enjoy… I just don’t always stay with them in a normal, easy way.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. So I wouldn’t say I don’t care about things I used to enjoy… I just don’t always stay with them in a normal, easy way.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you felt like you’ve lost interest in most of the things you used to enjoy, even outside of your art and the fair planning, like hobbies or time with friends?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 23 / not_in_workforce / associates\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think I’ve lost interest in most things, no. I mean, I’ve been less… I don’t know, easygoing about them, maybe.

Like with friends, if someone asks me to hang out, I might overthink it for a while, but once I’m actually there I can still enjoy parts of it. And hobbies are kind of the same — I’ll start something and then get distracted by whether I’m doing it “right,” which is annoying, because even making a playlist turns into this whole weird decision process sometimes.

But I wouldn’t say I just don’t care about any of it anymore. It’s more like my attention keeps getting hijacked.

Like, if I’m working on a design draft, part of me still likes the idea of it, but then I start thinking about how it’s going to look to the arts board, or whether I seem like I have everything together, and it kind of sucks the satisfaction out of it.\n\nAnd the fair planning notes — I’ll do some of them, but then I’ll notice I missed some small thing I normally would’ve handled, and I get guilty instead of feeling accomplished. Then I’ll end up canceling plans or putting off catching up, which makes it worse. So it’s not that I’ve stopped enjoying art completely, it’s more like since I tried to clean up my image and routine, everything around it feels tense and heavier than it used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’ve been moving more slowly than usual, or fidgety and restless lately, even when you’re not trying to be?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think I’ve really noticed that. I still care about stuff in flashes, like if a friend sends me a weird thrifted lamp or some cute room setup, I’ll have opinions immediately.\n\nBut then I start drifting into, like, “Should I be making my place look more pulled together?” And that’s not really the same as not caring, I guess. It just gets tangled up. So I wouldn’t say I don’t care about things I used to enjoy… I just don’t always stay with them in a normal, easy way.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like you’ve lost interest in most of the things you used to enjoy, even outside of your art and the fair planning, like hobbies or time with friends?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve lost interest in most things, no. I mean, I’ve been less… I don’t know, easygoing about them, maybe.\n\nLike with friends, if someone asks me to hang out, I might overthink it for a while, but once I’m actually there I can still enjoy parts of it. And hobbies are kind of the same — I’ll start something and then get distracted by whether I’m doing it “right,” which is annoying, because even making a playlist turns into this whole weird decision process sometimes.\n\nBut I wouldn’t say I just don’t care about any of it anymore. It’s more like my attention keeps getting hijacked.\n\n[Last Question]\nWould you say that you’ve lost interest in most of the things you used to enjoy, even the small ones, like hobbies, time with friends, or even things around your home?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },

I’m still not sure I’d call it “lost interest in most things.” Like, the small home stuff is actually where I can get weirdly interested — I’ll notice a corner looks messy and suddenly I’m looking at baskets online for 40 minutes, and then I’m not even sure if I wanted baskets or if I just got distracted.

Could you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Um, yeah… I came in because I’ve been trying to kind of clean up my image and my routine, mostly for this arts board stuff I’m involved with, and I thought that would make me feel more together, but it’s sort of done the opposite.\n\nI’m still doing the basics, like I’m not completely falling apart or anything, but I’ve been more tense and kind of down than I expected. And then I keep dropping these little things I normally care about—like fair planning notes, or design drafts I was supposed to work on—and then I feel guilty about it and end up canceling plans instead of just catching up. It’s frustrating because it’s not even huge things, but it keeps happening.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’re not enjoying things that you usually like to do, like working on your art or planning events?\n\nPatient: Yeah, kind of. I mean, I still care about the art stuff and the fair planning, it’s not like I suddenly hate it. It’s just been making me more tense and down than I expected.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how you feel when you're working on your art or planning events now compared to before you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Before, working on art or event stuff felt kind of energizing, like it gave me something to focus on. It’s weird because I’m still doing the basic day-to-day things, but these little things I normally care about keep slipping.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, and would you say your mood has been consistently low during this time?\n\nPatient: Um… I think it’s been about two months? At first I thought it was just the usual stress of changing habits, but it’s sort of stayed around.\n\nAnd the mood… I don’t know if I’d say low every single minute, but it’s been there a lot of the time since then, like this background heaviness.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any trouble concentrating or making decisions lately, especially when working on your art or planning events?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, kind of. Like with the design drafts or the fair planning notes, I’ll sit down and I know what I’m supposed to do, but then I get stuck thinking about whether I’m seeming put-together enough for the arts board, and suddenly the actual task feels way bigger than it is.\n\nIt’s not like I can’t function at all — I’m still doing the basic stuff. But these smaller things I normally care about keep slipping, and then I feel guilty, and instead of catching up I’ll cancel plans or avoid dealing with it. It’s just been more tense and heavy than I expected since I tried to clean up my image and routine.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling more tired or lacking energy more often than usual, even when you’re not physically exhausted?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I wouldn’t say I’m physically exhausted all the time or anything like that. Sometimes I feel kind of drained when I’m staring at what I need to do, but I don’t know if that’s “low energy” exactly or just me getting in my head.\n\nLike, I’ll sit there with my sketchbook open and then remember I still haven’t organized my supplies — which, honestly, I’ve been meaning to do for weeks because they’re all in this one bin from when I moved things around — and then I kind of lose momentum. But I haven’t really been tracking whether I’m more tired than usual.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that you’re not really interested in your art or event planning anymore, even when you have the time or opportunity to do it?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s that I don’t care about it anymore, exactly. Like, I still care about the art and the fair stuff — maybe too much, honestly — but since I tried to clean up my image and routine for the arts board, I’ve just been more tense and kind of down than I expected.\n\nSo I’ll have time to work on design drafts or fair planning notes, and instead of doing them I’ll get stuck, or drop some little thing I normally would’ve handled. Then I feel guilty about it, and sometimes I cancel plans instead of catching up. It’s frustrating because it’s not like I’ve stopped wanting to do it, I’m still doing the basics, but it feels harder to stay on top of the parts I used to be more excited about.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’re not getting as much enjoyment or satisfaction from your art or event planning as you used to, even when you do manage to work on them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess not as much, but it’s weird because I still do care about it. Like, if I’m working on a design draft, part of me still likes the idea of it, but then I start thinking about how it’s going to look to the arts board, or whether I seem like I have everything together, and it kind of sucks the satisfaction out of it.\n\nAnd the fair planning notes — I’ll do some of them, but then I’ll notice I missed some small thing I normally would’ve handled, and I get guilty instead of feeling accomplished. Then I’ll end up canceling plans or putting off catching up, which makes it worse. So it’s not that I’ve stopped enjoying art completely, it’s more like since I tried to clean up my image and routine, everything around it feels tense and heavier than it used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’ve been moving more slowly than usual, or fidgety and restless lately, even when you’re not trying to be?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think I’ve really noticed that. Like, I’m not sure I would catch myself moving slower unless someone pointed it out.\n\nSometimes I’ll get up to do something and then end up sort of wandering around my room because I see, like, a pile of receipts or an art supply I forgot to put away, and then I’m doing three little things instead of the one thing I meant to do. But I don’t know if that’s fidgety, exactly, or just me being distracted.\n\nI haven’t had anyone tell me I seem slowed down or restless, though. At least not that I can think of.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling like you're not good enough or that you're failing at things, especially related to how you're presenting yourself to the arts board?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say I think I’m failing at everything, but with the arts board stuff, yeah, it kind of gets in my head.\n\nLike, I came in because once I started trying to clean up my image and my routine for that, I got way more tense and down than I expected. I’m still doing the basic things, it’s not like I’ve totally fallen apart, but I keep dropping these little things I normally care about — fair planning notes, design draft details, stuff like that — and then I feel really guilty.\n\nAnd then instead of just catching up, I’ll cancel plans or avoid dealing with it for a bit, which makes me feel even more like I’m not presenting myself the way I should. It’s… frustrating.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like nothing really interests you or gives you pleasure anymore, even things you usually like doing outside of the arts board stuff?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say nothing interests me anymore. That sounds… bigger than what I mean, I think.\n\nLike, I still like things outside of all that, or at least I think I do. Sometimes I’ll start doing something random, like looking at little apartment decor ideas or watching someone paint online, and then somehow I’m thinking about whether my own workspace looks messy, which is not really the point. Sorry, that’s kind of a side thing.\n\nBut no, I haven’t really noticed that absolutely nothing gives me pleasure. It’s more hard for me to separate what I actually enjoy from all the pressure around looking like I’m doing okay.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself losing interest in almost all activities, even the ones you usually enjoy outside of your art and the fair planning?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure it’s almost all activities, no. And then I keep dropping these little things I normally care about—like fair planning notes, or design drafts I was supposed to work on—and then I feel guilty about it and end up canceling plans instead of just catching up. But these smaller things I normally care about keep slipping, and then I feel guilty, and instead of catching up I’ll cancel plans or avoid dealing with it. It’s just been more tense and heavy than I expected since I tried to clean up my image and routine.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling more tired or lacking energy more often than usual, even when you’re not physically exhausted?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I wouldn’t say I’m physically exhausted all the time or anything like that. Sometimes I feel kind of drained when I’m staring at what I need to do, but I don’t know if that’s “low energy” exactly or just me getting in my head.\n\nLike, I’ll sit there with my sketchbook open and then remember I still haven’t organized my supplies — which, honestly, I’ve been meaning to do for weeks because they’re all in this one bin from when I moved things around — and then I kind of lose momentum. But I haven’t really been tracking whether I’m more tired than usual.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that you’re not really interested in your art or event planning anymore, even when you have the time or opportunity to do it?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s that I don’t care about it anymore, exactly. Like, I still care about the art and the fair stuff — maybe too much, honestly — but since I tried to clean up my image and routine for the arts board, I’ve just been more tense and kind of down than I expected.\n\nSo I’ll have time to work on design drafts or fair planning notes, and instead of doing them I’ll get stuck, or drop some little thing I normally would’ve handled. Then I feel guilty about it, and sometimes I cancel plans instead of catching up. It’s frustrating because it’s not like I’ve stopped wanting to do it, I’m still doing the basics, but it feels harder to stay on top of the parts I used to be more excited about.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’re not getting as much enjoyment or satisfaction from your art or event planning as you used to, even when you do manage to work on them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess not as much, but it’s weird because I still do care about it. Like, if I’m working on a design draft, part of me still likes the idea of it, but then I start thinking about how it’s going to look to the arts board, or whether I seem like I have everything together, and it kind of sucks the satisfaction out of it.\n\nAnd the fair planning notes — I’ll do some of them, but then I’ll notice I missed some small thing I normally would’ve handled, and I get guilty instead of feeling accomplished. Then I’ll end up canceling plans or putting off catching up, which makes it worse. So it’s not that I’ve stopped enjoying art completely, it’s more like since I tried to clean up my image and routine, everything around it feels tense and heavier than it used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’ve been moving more slowly than usual, or fidgety and restless lately, even when you’re not trying to be?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think I’ve really noticed that. Like, I’m not sure I would catch myself moving slower unless someone pointed it out.\n\nSometimes I’ll get up to do something and then end up sort of wandering around my room because I see, like, a pile of receipts or an art supply I forgot to put away, and then I’m doing three little things instead of the one thing I meant to do. But I don’t know if that’s fidgety, exactly, or just me being distracted.\n\nI haven’t had anyone tell me I seem slowed down or restless, though. At least not that I can think of.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling like you're not good enough or that you're failing at things, especially related to how you're presenting yourself to the arts board?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say I think I’m failing at everything, but with the arts board stuff, yeah, it kind of gets in my head.\n\nLike, I came in because once I started trying to clean up my image and my routine for that, I got way more tense and down than I expected. I’m still doing the basic things, it’s not like I’ve totally fallen apart, but I keep dropping these little things I normally care about — fair planning notes, design draft details, stuff like that — and then I feel really guilty.\n\nAnd then instead of just catching up, I’ll cancel plans or avoid dealing with it for a bit, which makes me feel even more like I’m not presenting myself the way I should. It’s… frustrating.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like nothing really interests you or gives you pleasure anymore, even things you usually like doing outside of the arts board stuff?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say nothing interests me anymore. That sounds… bigger than what I mean, I think.\n\nLike, I still like things outside of all that, or at least I think I do. Sometimes I’ll start doing something random, like looking at little apartment decor ideas or watching someone paint online, and then somehow I’m thinking about whether my own workspace looks messy, which is not really the point. Sorry, that’s kind of a side thing.\n\nBut no, I haven’t really noticed that absolutely nothing gives me pleasure. It’s more hard for me to separate what I actually enjoy from all the pressure around looking like I’m doing okay.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself losing interest in almost all activities, even the ones you usually enjoy outside of your art and the fair planning?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure it’s almost all activities, no. I mean… I still get pulled into things, like if I see a video about someone making tiny ceramics or reorganizing a shelf in a really satisfying way, I’ll watch it longer than I meant to.\n\nBut then I start thinking, “Oh, my shelves look kind of chaotic,” and then it turns into this whole thing in my head instead of just enjoying it. So I guess I’m not sure if that counts as losing interest, or just not being able to stay with the enjoyment without it turning into… I don’t know, another comparison thing.\n\nI haven’t noticed that everything feels flat, though.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like you don’t really care about doing most of the things you used to enjoy, even outside of your art and the fair planning?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s most things, exactly. Or… I’m not sure. I still care about stuff in flashes, like if a friend sends me a weird thrifted lamp or some cute room setup, I’ll have opinions immediately.\n\nBut then I start drifting into, like, “Should I be making my place look more pulled together?” And that’s not really the same as not caring, I guess. It just gets tangled up. So I wouldn’t say I don’t care about things I used to enjoy… I just don’t always stay with them in a normal, easy way.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like you’ve lost interest in most of the things you used to enjoy, even outside of your art and the fair planning, like hobbies or time with friends?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve lost interest in most things, no. It’s more like my attention keeps getting hijacked.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you’ve lost interest in most of the things you used to enjoy, even the small ones, like hobbies, time with friends, or even things around your home?\n\nPatient: I’m still not sure I’d call it “lost interest in most things.” Like, the small home stuff is actually where I can get weirdly interested — I’ll notice a corner looks messy and suddenly I’m looking at baskets online for 40 minutes, and then I’m not even sure if I wanted baskets or if I just got distracted.\n\nWith friends, too, it’s not like I don’t want to see them. I can still have a good time once I’m there. I guess it just takes more mental… sorting? But I don’t know if that’s slowed down exactly. Physically, I don’t think anyone would say I’m moving weirdly slow or anything. I don’t think it’s like nothing brings me pleasure, no. I mean, “real satisfaction” is a hard phrase because sometimes I’ll enjoy something and then immediately start picking it apart, like, was that actually relaxing or did I just spend an hour arranging things on my phone?

But I still get enjoyment from things. Like if I’m with a friend and we end up laughing about something dumb, that still feels good. Or if something at home looks nicer after I fuss with it, I can feel satisfied for a bit. It just doesn’t always stay simple in my head, if that makes sense.

Like, I still care about the art and the fair stuff — maybe too much, honestly — but since I tried to clean up my image and routine for the arts board, I’ve just been more tense and kind of down than I expected.\n\nSo I’ll have time to work on design drafts or fair planning notes, and instead of doing them I’ll get stuck, or drop some little thing I normally would’ve handled. Then I feel guilty about it, and sometimes I cancel plans instead of catching up. It’s frustrating because it’s not like I’ve stopped wanting to do it, I’m still doing the basics, but it feels harder to stay on top of the parts I used to be more excited about.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’re not getting as much enjoyment or satisfaction from your art or event planning as you used to, even when you do manage to work on them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess not as much, but it’s weird because I still do care about it. Like, if I’m working on a design draft, part of me still likes the idea of it, but then I start thinking about how it’s going to look to the arts board, or whether I seem like I have everything together, and it kind of sucks the satisfaction out of it.\n\nAnd the fair planning notes — I’ll do some of them, but then I’ll notice I missed some small thing I normally would’ve handled, and I get guilty instead of feeling accomplished. Then I’ll end up canceling plans or putting off catching up, which makes it worse. So it’s not that I’ve stopped enjoying art completely, it’s more like since I tried to clean up my image and routine, everything around it feels tense and heavier than it used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’ve been moving more slowly than usual, or fidgety and restless lately, even when you’re not trying to be?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think I’ve really noticed that. Like, I’m not sure I would catch myself moving slower unless someone pointed it out.\n\nSometimes I’ll get up to do something and then end up sort of wandering around my room because I see, like, a pile of receipts or an art supply I forgot to put away, and then I’m doing three little things instead of the one thing I meant to do. But I don’t know if that’s fidgety, exactly, or just me being distracted.\n\nI haven’t had anyone tell me I seem slowed down or restless, though. At least not that I can think of.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling like you're not good enough or that you're failing at things, especially related to how you're presenting yourself to the arts board?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say I think I’m failing at everything, but with the arts board stuff, yeah, it kind of gets in my head.\n\nLike, I came in because once I started trying to clean up my image and my routine for that, I got way more tense and down than I expected. I’m still doing the basic things, it’s not like I’ve totally fallen apart, but I keep dropping these little things I normally care about — fair planning notes, design draft details, stuff like that — and then I feel really guilty.\n\nAnd then instead of just catching up, I’ll cancel plans or avoid dealing with it for a bit, which makes me feel even more like I’m not presenting myself the way I should. It’s… frustrating.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like nothing really interests you or gives you pleasure anymore, even things you usually like doing outside of the arts board stuff?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say nothing interests me anymore. That sounds… bigger than what I mean, I think.\n\nLike, I still like things outside of all that, or at least I think I do. Sometimes I’ll start doing something random, like looking at little apartment decor ideas or watching someone paint online, and then somehow I’m thinking about whether my own workspace looks messy, which is not really the point. Sorry, that’s kind of a side thing.\n\nBut no, I haven’t really noticed that absolutely nothing gives me pleasure. It’s more hard for me to separate what I actually enjoy from all the pressure around looking like I’m doing okay.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself losing interest in almost all activities, even the ones you usually enjoy outside of your art and the fair planning?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure it’s almost all activities, no. I mean… I still get pulled into things, like if I see a video about someone making tiny ceramics or reorganizing a shelf in a really satisfying way, I’ll watch it longer than I meant to.\n\nBut then I start thinking, “Oh, my shelves look kind of chaotic,” and then it turns into this whole thing in my head instead of just enjoying it. So I guess I’m not sure if that counts as losing interest, or just not being able to stay with the enjoyment without it turning into… I don’t know, another comparison thing.\n\nI haven’t noticed that everything feels flat, though.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like you don’t really care about doing most of the things you used to enjoy, even outside of your art and the fair planning?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s most things, exactly. Or… I’m not sure. I still care about stuff in flashes, like if a friend sends me a weird thrifted lamp or some cute room setup, I’ll have opinions immediately.\n\nBut then I start drifting into, like, “Should I be making my place look more pulled together?” And that’s not really the same as not caring, I guess. It just gets tangled up. So I wouldn’t say I don’t care about things I used to enjoy… I just don’t always stay with them in a normal, easy way.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like you’ve lost interest in most of the things you used to enjoy, even outside of your art and the fair planning, like hobbies or time with friends?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve lost interest in most things, no. I mean, I’ve been less… I don’t know, easygoing about them, maybe.\n\nLike with friends, if someone asks me to hang out, I might overthink it for a while, but once I’m actually there I can still enjoy parts of it. And hobbies are kind of the same — I’ll start something and then get distracted by whether I’m doing it “right,” which is annoying, because even making a playlist turns into this whole weird decision process sometimes.\n\nBut I wouldn’t say I just don’t care about any of it anymore. It’s more like my attention keeps getting hijacked.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you’ve lost interest in most of the things you used to enjoy, even the small ones, like hobbies, time with friends, or even things around your home?\n\nPatient: I’m still not sure I’d call it “lost interest in most things.” Like, the small home stuff is actually where I can get weirdly interested — I’ll notice a corner looks messy and suddenly I’m looking at baskets online for 40 minutes, and then I’m not even sure if I wanted baskets or if I just got distracted.\n\nWith friends, too, it’s not like I don’t want to see them. I can still have a good time once I’m there. I guess it just takes more mental… sorting? But no, I haven’t really noticed that most things I used to enjoy feel totally uninteresting.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your thoughts are slowing down or that you're physically moving more slowly than usual, or on the flip side, that you're more restless or fidgety than before, even when you're not trying to be?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve noticed that, not in a clear way. Like, I fidget sometimes, but I’ve always kind of done that — tapping my foot or messing with a sleeve or whatever.\n\nThe “thoughts slowing down” thing is harder for me to tell, because sometimes I’ll sit there deciding between two stupidly similar things, like which mug to use, and it feels like my brain is being dramatic. But I don’t know if that’s slowed down exactly. Physically, I don’t think anyone would say I’m moving weirdly slow or anything. Restless maybe here and there, but not in a way that feels new enough that I’d be like, “Oh, that’s a symptom.”\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like nothing you do brings you any real pleasure or satisfaction most of the time, even things you normally enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s like nothing brings me pleasure, no. I mean, “real satisfaction” is a hard phrase because sometimes I’ll enjoy something and then immediately start picking it apart, like, was that actually relaxing or did I just spend an hour arranging things on my phone?\n\nBut I still get enjoyment from things. Like if I’m with a friend and we end up laughing about something dumb, that still feels good. Or if something at home looks nicer after I fuss with it, I can feel satisfied for a bit. It just doesn’t always stay simple in my head, if that makes sense.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Would you say that you’ve lost interest in most of the things you used to enjoy, even the small ones, like hobbies, time with friends, or even things around your home?\n\nPatient: I’m still not sure I’d call it “lost interest in most things.” Like, the small home stuff is actually where I can get weirdly interested — I’ll notice a corner looks messy and suddenly I’m looking at baskets online for 40 minutes, and then I’m not even sure if I wanted baskets or if I just got distracted.\n\nWith friends, too, it’s not like I don’t want to see them. I can still have a good time once I’m there. I guess it just takes more mental… sorting? But no, I haven’t really noticed that most things I used to enjoy feel totally uninteresting.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your thoughts are slowing down or that you're physically moving more slowly than usual, or on the flip side, that you're more restless or fidgety than before, even when you're not trying to be?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve noticed that, not in a clear way. Like, I fidget sometimes, but I’ve always kind of done that — tapping my foot or messing with a sleeve or whatever.\n\nThe “thoughts slowing down” thing is harder for me to tell, because sometimes I’ll sit there deciding between two stupidly similar things, like which mug to use, and it feels like my brain is being dramatic. But I don’t know if that’s slowed down exactly. Physically, I don’t think anyone would say I’m moving weirdly slow or anything. Restless maybe here and there, but not in a way that feels new enough that I’d be like, “Oh, that’s a symptom.”\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like nothing you do brings you any real pleasure or satisfaction most of the time, even things you normally enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s like nothing brings me pleasure, no. Like if I’m with a friend and we end up laughing about something dumb, that still feels good. Or if something at home looks nicer after I fuss with it, I can feel satisfied for a bit. It just doesn’t always stay simple in my head, if that makes sense.\n\n[Last Question]\nWould you say that you’ve lost interest in most of your usual activities, even the ones you used to enjoy the most, like your art, time with friends, or hobbies around home?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 23 / not_in_workforce / associates\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there.     "role": "user",

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure I’d say I’ve lost interest in most of them. Like with art, sometimes I’ll avoid starting because it feels like this whole thing, and then I end up reorganizing my pens or watching a video about someone else’s sketchbook instead, which is not really the same as doing it.

But when I actually sit down with it, I can still get into it sometimes. Same with friends — I might drag my feet getting there, but then I’m usually glad I went. And home stuff, honestly, I still get pulled into that pretty easily. So… no, I don’t think it’s a big loss of interest, more like I’m a little scattered about starting things.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.9, F33.0, F43.22\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Could you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Um, yeah… I came in because I’ve been trying to kind of clean up my image and my routine, mostly for this arts board stuff I’m involved with, and I thought that would make me feel more together, but it’s sort of done the opposite.\n\nI’m still doing the basics, like I’m not completely falling apart or anything, but I’ve been more tense and kind of down than I expected. And then I keep dropping these little things I normally care about—like fair planning notes, or design drafts I was supposed to work on—and then I feel guilty about it and end up canceling plans instead of just catching up. It’s frustrating because it’s not even huge things, but it keeps happening.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’re not enjoying things that you usually like to do, like working on your art or planning events?\n\nPatient: Yeah, kind of. I mean, I still care about the art stuff and the fair planning, it’s not like I suddenly hate it. But since I started trying to make myself look more put-together for the arts board, everything around it feels heavier.\n\nLike, I’ll have design drafts I meant to work on, or notes for the fair, and then I just… don’t do them, or I put them off. And then I feel guilty, so instead of catching up or going to meet people, I’ll cancel plans. Which is dumb, because those are usually the things I like, you know? It’s just been making me more tense and down than I expected.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how you feel when you're working on your art or planning events now compared to before you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Before, working on art or event stuff felt kind of energizing, like it gave me something to focus on. Now it still matters to me, but it feels heavier. Like I’ll sit down with design drafts or fair planning notes and suddenly I’m thinking about whether I seem organized enough for the arts board, or if I’m messing up some image I’m supposed to have.\n\nAnd then I get tense, and if I don’t get it done I feel guilty, and instead of just picking it back up later I’ll cancel plans or avoid people. It’s weird because I’m still doing the basic day-to-day things, but these little things I normally care about keep slipping.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, and would you say your mood has been consistently low during this time?\n\nPatient: Um… I think it’s been about two months? It kind of started when I decided I needed to overhaul my routine and how I present myself, like trying to seem more polished for the arts board. At first I thought it was just the usual stress of changing habits, but it’s sort of stayed around.\n\nAnd the mood… I don’t know if I’d say low every single minute, but it’s been there a lot of the time since then, like this background heaviness.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any trouble concentrating or making decisions lately, especially when working on your art or planning events?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, kind of. Like with the design drafts or the fair planning notes, I’ll sit down and I know what I’m supposed to do, but then I get stuck thinking about whether I’m seeming put-together enough for the arts board, and suddenly the actual task feels way bigger than it is.\n\nIt’s not like I can’t function at all — I’m still doing the basic stuff. But these smaller things I normally care about keep slipping, and then I feel guilty, and instead of catching up I’ll cancel plans or avoid dealing with it. It’s just been more tense and heavy than I expected since I tried to clean up my image and routine.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling more tired or lacking energy more often than usual, even when you’re not physically exhausted?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I wouldn’t say I’m physically exhausted all the time or anything like that. Sometimes I feel kind of drained when I’m staring at what I need to do, but I don’t know if that’s “low energy” exactly or just me getting in my head.\n\nLike, I’ll sit there with my sketchbook open and then remember I still haven’t organized my supplies — which, honestly, I’ve been meaning to do for weeks because they’re all in this one bin from when I moved things around — and then I kind of lose momentum. But I haven’t really been tracking whether I’m more tired than usual.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that you’re not really interested in your art or event planning anymore, even when you have the time or opportunity to do it?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s that I don’t care about it anymore, exactly. Like, I still care about the art and the fair stuff — maybe too much, honestly — but since I tried to clean up my image and routine for the arts board, I’ve just been more tense and kind of down than I expected.\n\nSo I’ll have time to work on design drafts or fair planning notes, and instead of doing them I’ll get stuck, or drop some little thing I normally would’ve handled. Then I feel guilty about it, and sometimes I cancel plans instead of catching up. It’s frustrating because it’s not like I’ve stopped wanting to do it, I’m still doing the basics, but it feels harder to stay on top of the parts I used to be more excited about.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’re not getting as much enjoyment or satisfaction from your art or event planning as you used to, even when you do manage to work on them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess not as much, but it’s weird because I still do care about it. Like, if I’m working on a design draft, part of me still likes the idea of it, but then I start thinking about how it’s going to look to the arts board, or whether I seem like I have everything together, and it kind of sucks the satisfaction out of it.\n\nAnd the fair planning notes — I’ll do some of them, but then I’ll notice I missed some small thing I normally would’ve handled, and I get guilty instead of feeling accomplished. Then I’ll end up canceling plans or putting off catching up, which makes it worse. So it’s not that I’ve stopped enjoying art completely, it’s more like since I tried to clean up my image and routine, everything around it feels tense and heavier than it used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’ve been moving more slowly than usual, or fidgety and restless lately, even when you’re not trying to be?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think I’ve really noticed that. Like, I’m not sure I would catch myself moving slower unless someone pointed it out.\n\nSometimes I’ll get up to do something and then end up sort of wandering around my room because I see, like, a pile of receipts or an art supply I forgot to put away, and then I’m doing three little things instead of the one thing I meant to do. But I don’t know if that’s fidgety, exactly, or just me being distracted.\n\nI haven’t had anyone tell me I seem slowed down or restless, though. At least not that I can think of.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling like you're not good enough or that you're failing at things, especially related to how you're presenting yourself to the arts board?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say I think I’m failing at everything, but with the arts board stuff, yeah, it kind of gets in my head.\n\nLike, I came in because once I started trying to clean up my image and my routine for that, I got way more tense and down than I expected. I’m still doing the basic things, it’s not like I’ve totally fallen apart, but I keep dropping these little things I normally care about — fair planning notes, design draft details, stuff like that — and then I feel really guilty.\n\nAnd then instead of just catching up, I’ll cancel plans or avoid dealing with it for a bit, which makes me feel even more like I’m not presenting myself the way I should. It’s… frustrating.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like nothing really interests you or gives you pleasure anymore, even things you usually like doing outside of the arts board stuff?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say nothing interests me anymore. That sounds… bigger than what I mean, I think.\n\nLike, I still like things outside of all that, or at least I think I do. Sometimes I’ll start doing something random, like looking at little apartment decor ideas or watching someone paint online, and then somehow I’m thinking about whether my own workspace looks messy, which is not really the point. Sorry, that’s kind of a side thing.\n\nBut no, I haven’t really noticed that absolutely nothing gives me pleasure. It’s more hard for me to separate what I actually enjoy from all the pressure around looking like I’m doing okay.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself losing interest in almost all activities, even the ones you usually enjoy outside of your art and the fair planning?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure it’s almost all activities, no. I mean… I still get pulled into things, like if I see a video about someone making tiny ceramics or reorganizing a shelf in a really satisfying way, I’ll watch it longer than I meant to.\n\nBut then I start thinking, “Oh, my shelves look kind of chaotic,” and then it turns into this whole thing in my head instead of just enjoying it. So I guess I’m not sure if that counts as losing interest, or just not being able to stay with the enjoyment without it turning into… I don’t know, another comparison thing.\n\nI haven’t noticed that everything feels flat, though.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like you don’t really care about doing most of the things you used to enjoy, even outside of your art and the fair planning?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s most things, exactly. Or… I’m not sure. I still care about stuff in flashes, like if a friend sends me a weird thrifted lamp or some cute room setup, I’ll have opinions immediately.\n\nBut then I start drifting into, like, “Should I be making my place look more pulled together?” And that’s not really the same as not caring, I guess. It just gets tangled up. So I wouldn’t say I don’t care about things I used to enjoy… I just don’t always stay with them in a normal, easy way.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like you’ve lost interest in most of the things you used to enjoy, even outside of your art and the fair planning, like hobbies or time with friends?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve lost interest in most things, no. I mean, I’ve been less… I don’t know, easygoing about them, maybe.\n\nLike with friends, if someone asks me to hang out, I might overthink it for a while, but once I’m actually there I can still enjoy parts of it. And hobbies are kind of the same — I’ll start something and then get distracted by whether I’m doing it “right,” which is annoying, because even making a playlist turns into this whole weird decision process sometimes.\n\nBut I wouldn’t say I just don’t care about any of it anymore. It’s more like my attention keeps getting hijacked.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you’ve lost interest in most of the things you used to enjoy, even the small ones, like hobbies, time with friends, or even things around your home?\n\nPatient: I’m still not sure I’d call it “lost interest in most things.” Like, the small home stuff is actually where I can get weirdly interested — I’ll notice a corner looks messy and suddenly I’m looking at baskets online for 40 minutes, and then I’m not even sure if I wanted baskets or if I just got distracted.\n\nWith friends, too, it’s not like I don’t want to see them. Restless maybe here and there, but not in a way that feels new enough that I’d be like, “Oh, that’s a symptom.”\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like nothing you do brings you any real pleasure or satisfaction most of the time, even things you normally enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s like nothing brings me pleasure, no. So I wouldn’t say I don’t care about things I used to enjoy… I just don’t always stay with them in a normal, easy way.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like you’ve lost interest in most of the things you used to enjoy, even outside of your art and the fair planning, like hobbies or time with friends?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve lost interest in most things, no. I mean, I’ve been less… I don’t know, easygoing about them, maybe.\n\nLike with friends, if someone asks me to hang out, I might overthink it for a while, but once I’m actually there I can still enjoy parts of it. And hobbies are kind of the same — I’ll start something and then get distracted by whether I’m doing it “right,” which is annoying, because even making a playlist turns into this whole weird decision process sometimes.\n\nBut I wouldn’t say I just don’t care about any of it anymore. It’s more like my attention keeps getting hijacked.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you’ve lost interest in most of the things you used to enjoy, even the small ones, like hobbies, time with friends, or even things around your home?\n\nPatient: I’m still not sure I’d call it “lost interest in most things.” Like, the small home stuff is actually where I can get weirdly interested — I’ll notice a corner looks messy and suddenly I’m looking at baskets online for 40 minutes, and then I’m not even sure if I wanted baskets or if I just got distracted.\n\nWith friends, too, it’s not like I don’t want to see them. But no, I haven’t really noticed that most things I used to enjoy feel totally uninteresting.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your thoughts are slowing down or that you're physically moving more slowly than usual, or on the flip side, that you're more restless or fidgety than before, even when you're not trying to be?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve noticed that, not in a clear way. Like, I fidget sometimes, but I’ve always kind of done that — tapping my foot or messing with a sleeve or whatever.\n\nThe “thoughts slowing down” thing is harder for me to tell, because sometimes I’ll sit there deciding between two stupidly similar things, like which mug to use, and it feels like my brain is being dramatic. But I don’t know if that’s slowed down exactly. Physically, I don’t think anyone would say I’m moving weirdly slow or anything. Restless maybe here and there, but not in a way that feels new enough that I’d be like, “Oh, that’s a symptom.”\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like nothing you do brings you any real pleasure or satisfaction most of the time, even things you normally enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s like nothing brings me pleasure, no. It just doesn’t always stay simple in my head, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you’ve lost interest in most of your usual activities, even the ones you used to enjoy the most, like your art, time with friends, or hobbies around home?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’ve lost interest in most of them. Most of the time… I don’t know if I’d say even when things are going fine, exactly. It’s more like since I tried to clean up my whole image and routine for the arts board stuff, I’ve been more tense and kind of down than I expected.

    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I mean, I’m still functioning, like I’m not just staying in bed or missing basic errands. I can still get groceries and show up to things I really have to show up for.

But it has affected me in the areas people usually rely on me for, which is the part that makes me feel bad. Like with the fair planning stuff, or design follow-through, I’ve been… less dependable than I usually am. I’ll tell myself I’m going to send the notes or finish a draft, and then I get weirdly stuck, and then I’m late or I have to apologize. And then I start thinking about how everyone probably sees me as flaky, which is ironic because I used to be the person who cared too much about those details.

So, most days maybe not in this dramatic way, but yes, it’s been enough that I’m not doing as well in the volunteer and creative things people count on me for.

It’s more like my attention keeps getting hijacked.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you’ve lost interest in most of the things you used to enjoy, even the small ones, like hobbies, time with friends, or even things around your home?\n\nPatient: I’m still not sure I’d call it “lost interest in most things.” Like, the small home stuff is actually where I can get weirdly interested — I’ll notice a corner looks messy and suddenly I’m looking at baskets online for 40 minutes, and then I’m not even sure if I wanted baskets or if I just got distracted.\n\nWith friends, too, it’s not like I don’t want to see them. I can still have a good time once I’m there. I guess it just takes more mental… sorting? But no, I haven’t really noticed that most things I used to enjoy feel totally uninteresting.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your thoughts are slowing down or that you're physically moving more slowly than usual, or on the flip side, that you're more restless or fidgety than before, even when you're not trying to be?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve noticed that, not in a clear way. Like, I fidget sometimes, but I’ve always kind of done that — tapping my foot or messing with a sleeve or whatever.\n\nThe “thoughts slowing down” thing is harder for me to tell, because sometimes I’ll sit there deciding between two stupidly similar things, like which mug to use, and it feels like my brain is being dramatic. But I don’t know if that’s slowed down exactly. Physically, I don’t think anyone would say I’m moving weirdly slow or anything. Restless maybe here and there, but not in a way that feels new enough that I’d be like, “Oh, that’s a symptom.”\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like nothing you do brings you any real pleasure or satisfaction most of the time, even things you normally enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s like nothing brings me pleasure, no. I mean, “real satisfaction” is a hard phrase because sometimes I’ll enjoy something and then immediately start picking it apart, like, was that actually relaxing or did I just spend an hour arranging things on my phone?\n\nBut I still get enjoyment from things. Like if I’m with a friend and we end up laughing about something dumb, that still feels good. Or if something at home looks nicer after I fuss with it, I can feel satisfied for a bit. It just doesn’t always stay simple in my head, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you’ve lost interest in most of your usual activities, even the ones you used to enjoy the most, like your art, time with friends, or hobbies around home?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’ve lost interest in most of them. Like with art, sometimes I’ll avoid starting because it feels like this whole thing, and then I end up reorganizing my pens or watching a video about someone else’s sketchbook instead, which is not really the same as doing it.\n\nBut when I actually sit down with it, I can still get into it sometimes. Same with friends — I might drag my feet getting there, but then I’m usually glad I went. And home stuff, honestly, I still get pulled into that pretty easily. So… no, I don’t think it’s a big loss of interest, more like I’m a little scattered about starting things.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that most of the time, even when things are going fine, you still feel down or low in mood?\n\nPatient: Most of the time… I don’t know if I’d say even when things are going fine, exactly. It’s more like since I tried to clean up my whole image and routine for the arts board stuff, I’ve been more tense and kind of down than I expected.\n\nLike, I’m still doing the basics. I’m getting through the day. But then I drop these little things I normally care about — fair planning notes, or design drafts, stuff like that — and then I feel guilty, and instead of just catching up I’ll cancel plans. Which sounds silly when I say it, because it’s not like the world ends if a draft is late, but it kind of snowballs in my head. So… not constantly low for no reason, but I have been lower than usual around all of that.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your mood has been low or down most days, even when nothing particularly bad is happening, and that this has affected how you function in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I’m still functioning, like I’m not just staying in bed or missing basic errands. I can still get groceries and show up to things I really have to show up for.\n\nBut it has affected me in the areas people usually rely on me for, which is the part that makes me feel bad. Like with the fair planning stuff, or design follow-through, I’ve been… less dependable than I usually am. I’ll tell myself I’m going to send the notes or finish a draft, and then I get weirdly stuck, and then I’m late or I have to apologize. And then I start thinking about how everyone probably sees me as flaky, which is ironic because I used to be the person who cared too much about those details.\n\nSo, most days maybe not in this dramatic way, but yes, it’s been enough that I’m not doing as well in the volunteer and creative things people count on me for."
